On Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements having no reversal factorization
Tommy Parisi, Mark Skandera, Ben Spahiu, Jiayuan Wang

Abstract
. For w w in the symmetric group 𝔖 n \mathfrak{S}_{n} , let C ~ w \widetilde{C}_{w} be the corresponding modified, signless Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element of the type- 𝖠 \mathsf{A} Hecke algebra H n ​ ( q ) H_{n}(q) . An extension [ Ann. Comb. 25 , no. 3 (2021) pp. 757–787] of a result of Deodhar [ Geom. Dedicata 36 , (1990) pp. 95–119] implies that any factorization of the form C ~ w = 1 f ⁡ ( q ) C ~ v ( 1 ) ⋯ C ~ v ( r ) , \widetilde{C}_{w}=\frac{1}{f(q)}\widetilde{C}_{v^{(1)}}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{v^{(r)}}, with v ( 1 ) , … , v ( r ) v^{(1)},\dotsc,v^{(r)} maximal elements of parabolic subgroups of 𝔖 n \mathfrak{S}_{n} and f ⁡ ( q ) ∈ ℕ ⁡ [ q ] f(q)\in\mathbb{N}[q] depending on these, provides cancellation-free combinatorial interpretations of the polynomials { P v , w ​ ( q ) | v ∈ 𝔖 n } \{P_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\} appearing in the expansion ∑ v P v , w ​ ( q ) ​ T v \sum_{v}P_{v,w}(q)T_{v} of C ~ w \widetilde{C}_{w} in terms of the natural basis { T v | v ∈ 𝔖 n } \{T_{v}\,|\,v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\} of H n ​ ( q ) H_{n}(q) . While the set of permutations w ∈ 𝔖 n w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n} admitting such a factorization of C ~ w \smash{\widetilde{C}_{w}} has not yet been characterized, we apply a result of Gaetz – Gao [ Adv. Math. 457 (2024) Paper No. 109941] to describe a set admitting no such factorization.



1.
Introduction

The
Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials

\{P_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}[q]
are entries of the change-of-basis matrix relating a certain
Kazhdan–Lusztig basis
of the Hecke algebra with another
natural basis.

First appearing in the study of representations of the Hecke algebra,
they were given existential and recursive definitions in
[
20
]
.
Appearances of the polynomials in other areas such as Lie Theory
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
9
]
,
quantum groups
[
14
]
,
combinatorics
[
18
]
,
and Schubert varieties
[
20
]
,
[
21
]

have inspired a search for simpler descriptions.
Ideally, such a description should interpret each coefficient of
P_{v,w}(q)
as a set cardinality.

Some
famous
alternative formulas for the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials are due to Brenti and Deodhar. Brenti expressed
P_{v,w}(q)
in two different ways

[
7
, §3]
,
[
8
, §3]

as simple linear combinations of recursively defined polynomials in
\mathbb{Z}[q]
having both positive and negative
coefficients.
Because of negative coefficients and recursive definitions, these formulas do not interpret coefficients in
P_{v,w}(q)
as set cardinalities. Deodhar
[
13
]
developed an algorithm which takes any reduced expression for
w
as an input, and
outputs
a set
\mathscr{E}_{\min}
of (not necessarily reduced) expressions
for other permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. For each
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
k>0
, the coefficient of
q^{k}
in
P_{v,w}(q)
is equal to the cardinality of a certain subset of
\mathscr{E}_{\min}
. On the other hand, the algorithmic component of Deodhar’s method makes it difficult to apply his combinatorial interpretation in practice.

Billey and Warrington showed
[
4
, Thm. 1, Rmk. 6]
that
when
w
has certain properties, Deodhar’s algorithm is trivial, and the output set

\mathscr{E}_{\min}
of expressions can be replaced by a more visually appealing set of path families in a certain wiring diagram.
Again for each
v
and
k
, the coefficient of
q^{k}
in
P_{v,w}(q)
is equal to the cardinality of a subset of these path families. Clearwater and the second author
[
11
, Cor. 5.3]
then extended this result to permutations
w
for which the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\smash{\widetilde{C}_{w}}
factors nicely, but did not solve the problem
[
24
, Quest. 4.5]
of characterizing such permutations
w
.

In Sections
2
–
3
we review basic facts and results about
the symmetric group, planar networks, the Hecke algebra, and the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis and polynomials.
In Section
4
we use the result
[
11
, Cor. 5.3]
to state properties of polynomials which arise in the natural expansion of products of certain Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements of the Hecke algebra. This leads to a partial answer in Section
5
to the characterization question
[
24
, Quest. 4.5]
: a description of
certain Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements which do not factor as desired.

2.
The symmetric group and planar networks

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be the symmetric group, with standard generators

s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n-1}
, length function
\ell
, and Bruhat order
\leq
.
(See, e.g.,
[
6
]
for definitions.)
Given a word
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{k}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
and a word
y=y_{1}\cdots y_{k}
having
k
distinct letters,
we say that
y
matches the pattern
u
if the letters of
y
appear
in the same relative order as those of
u
; that is, if we have

u_{i}<u_{j}
if and only if
y_{i}<y_{j}
for all
i,j\in[k]:=\{1,\dotsc,k\}
.
On the other hand,
say that
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

avoids the pattern
u
if no subword
of
w
matches the pattern
u
.

It is easy to see that for
each subinterval
[a,b]:=\{a,\dotsc,b\}
of
[n]
,
the

reversal

(2.1)

s_{[a,b]}:=1\cdots(a-1)b\cdots a(b+1)\cdots n\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
This element
is the unique longest (maximum length) element of the subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
generated by
s_{a},\dotsc,s_{b-1}
.
More generally, each
parabolic
subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
generated by a subset
of generators
has longest element equal to a product of reversals on disjoint intervals.
Multiplication of reversals in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
or of related elements

(2.2)

D_{[a,b]}:=\negthickspace\sum_{v\leq s_{[a,b]}}\negthickspace v

in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}]
can be performed graphically with certain planar networks.

Define a
planar network of order n
to be a directed, planar, acyclic multigraph with
2n
boundary vertices having
n
source vertices on the left and
n
sink vertices on the right, both labeled
1,\dotsc,n
from bottom to top.
We will allow edges
({x},{y})

to be marked by a positive integer multiplicity
\mu(x,y)
.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{n}
denote the set of such networks.
For each subinterval
[a,b]
of
[n]
we define a
simple star network

\smash{F_{[a,b]}}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
by

(1)

an interior vertex
x
lies between the sources and sinks,

(2)

for
i\in[a,b]
we have edges from source
i
to
x
and from
x
to sink
i
,

(3)

for
i\notin[a,b]
we have edges from source
i
to sink
i
.

For example, the simple star network
F_{\smash{[2,4]}}\in\mathcal{G}_{4}
is

\phantom{\sum}\negthickspace F_{\emptyset}\phantom{\sum}\end{gathered}

Given networks
E,F\in\mathcal{G}_{n}

in which all sources have
outdegree
1
and all sinks have indegree
1
, define
the concatenation
E\circ F
of
E
and
F

as follows. For
i=1,\dotsc,n
, do

(1)

remove sink
i
of
E
and source
i
of
F
,

(2)

merge each edge
({x},\text{sink }i)
in
E
with each edge

(\text{source }i,{y})
in
F

to form a single edge
({x},{y})
in
E\circ F
.

Thus a concatenation of the form

F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}

has
2n+m
vertices:

n
sources inherited from
F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}
,
n
sinks inherited from
F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
, and
m
internal vertices
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}
, where
x_{j}
is inherited from
F_{[a_{j},b_{j}]}
.
Sometimes in a concatenation
E\circ F
,
there may exist internal vertices
{x}
in
E
,
{y}
in
F

with

\mu({x},{y})>1
multiplicity-
1
edges
incident upon both.
Define the
condensed concatenation

E\bullet F
to be the
subdigraph of
E\circ F
obtained
by removing, for all such pairs
({x},{y})
,
all but one of the
\mu({x},{y})
edges incident upon both,
and by marking this remaining edge with the multiplicity
\mu({x},{y})
.
For example, in
\mathcal{G}_{4}
we have the
graphs

(2.4)

F_{[1,3]}\circ F_{[2,4]}\circ F_{[1,3]}=\hbox to89.18pt{\vbox to62.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.98357pt\lower-31.1963pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.65813pt}{0.70033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{21.38896pt}{17.77216pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{43.58238pt}{0.70033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

in which the two multiplicity-
2
edges
(x_{1},x_{2})
,

(x_{2},x_{3})
of

F_{[1,3]}\bullet F_{[2,4]}\bullet F_{[1,3]}

are the remnants of pairs of edges incident upon the same
internal vertices in
F_{[1,3]}\circ F_{[2,4]}\circ F_{[1,3]}
.

Define a
star network
to be an element

F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}

of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
constructed by any combination of concatenation and condensed concatenation of finitely many simple star networks. Regardless of the types of concatenation used, let
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}

denote the central vertices inherited from the
m
simple star networks, and define
numbers

(2.5)

\mu(x_{i},x_{j})=\#\text{ edges incident upon both $x_{i}$ and $x_{j}$ in }F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the set of all star networks of order
n
.
For
F\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
, call a sequence
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n})

of source-to-sink paths in

F
a
path family of type
v=v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

if for all
i
, path
\pi_{i}
begins at source
i
and terminates at sink
v_{i}
. Say that
\pi

covers

F
if each multiplicity-
p
edge
f
in
F

belongs to

p
of the paths in
\pi
,
and define the sets

(2.6)

\begin{gathered}\Pi(F)=\{\pi\,|\,\pi\text{ a path family covering }F\},\\
\Pi_{v}(F)=\{\pi\in\Pi(F)\,|\,\mathrm{type}(\pi)=v\}.\end{gathered}

In terms of the definitions (
2.6
), we may combinatorially interpret products of elements (
2.2
) quite simply.
We say that
F

graphically represents

(2.7)

\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\negthickspace|\Pi_{v}(F)|\,v

as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}]
.
For

F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
,
this element is

D_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots D_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
;
for

F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
,
it is

D_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots D_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}

divided by the product, over all pairs
i<j
,
of the numbers
\mu(x_{i},x_{j})!
defined in (
2.5
).
In either case, we
see that the coefficient of
e
in (
2.7
) is at least
1
: the unique noncrossing path family covering
F
has type
e
.

3.
The Hecke algebra and planar networks

Define the
(type-
A
Iwahori-) Hecke algebra

H_{n}(q)

to be the

\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}},q^{\negthinspace\bar{\,}\frac{1}{2}}]
-span of its
natural basis

\{T_{w}\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}
,
with multiplication given by

T_{s_{i}}T_{w}=\begin{cases}T_{s_{i}w}&\text{if $s_{i}w>w$},\\
(q-1)T_{s_{i}w}+qT_{w}&\text{if $s_{i}w<w$}.\end{cases}

Specializing at
q^{\frac{1}{2}}=1
we have
H_{n}(1)\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}]

with
T_{w}\mapsto w
.

A semilinear involution on
H_{n}(q)
, known as the
bar involution
, is defined by

\overline{q^{\frac{1}{2}}}:=q^{\negthinspace\bar{\,}\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\overline{T_{w}}:=(T_{w^{-1}})^{-1},\qquad\overline{\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}B_{w}(q)T_{w}}:=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\overline{B_{w}(q)}\,\overline{T_{w}}.

Kazhdan and Lusztig showed
[
20
]
that
H_{n}(q)
has a unique
basis
\{C^{\prime}_{w}\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}

satisfying

\overline{C^{\prime}_{w}}=C^{\prime}_{w}
for all
w

and

(3.1)

q^{\frac{\ell(w)}{2}}C^{\prime}_{w}=\sum_{v\leq w}P_{v,w}(q)T_{w},

where coefficients
P_{v,w}(q)\in\mathbb{N}[q]
, known as the
Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
, have constant term
1
, satisfy

\deg(P_{v,w}(q))<\frac{\ell(w)-\ell(v)-1}{2}
for
v<w
,
and satisfy

P_{w,w}(q)=1
for all
w
.
We also have that if

w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
then
P_{v,w}(q)=1
for all
v\leq w

[
22
]
.
For convenience, we define

(3.2)

\widetilde{C}_{w}:=q^{\frac{\ell(w)}{2}}C^{\prime}_{w}

and work with this (modified)
Kazhdan–Lusztig basis
\{\widetilde{C}_{w}\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}
. These elements and their products
appear in various settings, including
intersection homology
[
1
]
,
[
26
]
,
algorithmic and combinatorial description of Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements themselves
[
4
]
,
[
13
]
,
Schubert varieties
[
4
]
, total nonnegativity
[
16
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
29
]
,
trace evaluations
[
10
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
25
]
,
and symmetric functions
[
10
]
,
[
18
]
,
[
25
]
.
Each element
D_{[a,b]}
in Section
2
is the
q^{\frac{1}{2}}=1
specialization of
\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a,b]}}
.

Deodhar
[
13
, Prop. 3.5]

studied sequences

(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{m}})

of generators of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
products of the
corresponding
Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements

\widetilde{C}_{\smash{s_{i_{j}}}}=T_{e}+T_{\smash{s_{i_{j}}}}
of
H_{n}(q)
,
and their natural expansions

(3.3)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{1}}\negthickspace}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{m}}\negthickspace}=\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}A_{v}(q)T_{v}.

He
described
the
resulting
coefficients
\{A_{v}(q)\,|\,v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{N}[q]

in terms of
subexpressions

of
(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{m}})
,
sequences
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{m})
with
\sigma_{j}\in\{e,s_{i_{j}}\}

for
j=1,\dotsc,m
.
(Our treatment here differs slightly from that of
[
13
]
but is equivalent.) Call index
j
a
defect
of
\sigma
if

(3.4)

\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j-1}s_{i_{j}}<\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j-1}

and let
\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)
denote the number of defects of
\sigma
.
(Observe that
j=1
cannot be a defect: we have
s_{i_{1}}>e
always.)
Each coefficient on the right-hand side of (
3.3
) is given by

(3.5)

A_{v}(q)=\sum_{\sigma}q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)},

where the sum is over all subexpressions

\sigma
of

(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{m}})

satisfying
\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m}=v
.

Billey and Warrington observed
[
4
, Rmk. 6]

that
the defect statistic has a simple graphical interpretation.
Specifically, subexpressions
of
(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{m}})
correspond bijectively to path families
covering

(3.6)

F=F_{[i_{1},i_{1}+1]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[i_{m},i_{m}+1]}

in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
with

(\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{m})
corresponding
to the family
\pi\in\Pi(F)

constructed by prescribing

\text{the paths meeting at ${x}_{j}$ }\begin{cases}\text{cross there}&\text{if $\sigma_{j}=s_{i_{j}}$},\\
\text{do not cross there}&\text{if $\sigma_{j}=e$}.\end{cases}

By this bijection, index
j
is a defect of
\sigma
in the sense of (
3.4
) if and only if the paths meeting at
{x}_{j}

have previously crossed an odd number of times.

Clearwater–Skandera
extended this
result
[
11
, Cor. 5.3]
to products of the form

(3.7)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}}=\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}A_{v}(q)T_{v},

where
each factor satisfies

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{j},b_{j}]}}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{u\leq s_{[a_{j},b_{j}]}}\negthickspace\negthickspace T_{u},

since reversals avoid the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
This extension requires a more general definition of defects.
While the intersection of two paths in
(
3.6
) is a union of vertices,
the intersection of two paths in a more general star network

F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}

is a subgraph of
F
whose connected components are vertices or are paths of the form

(3.8)

(x_{k},\dotsc,x_{\ell})

for some
k<\ell
. (For example, consider the unique noncrossing path family covering the second network in (
2.4
).) For each initial vertex
x_{k}
in a component (
3.8
) of the intersection of two paths, we will say that the paths
meet
at
x_{k}
.
Our embedding of star networks
in the plane naturally allows us to declare an edge entering (exiting) a vertex
x_{k}
to be above or below another edge entering (exiting)
x_{k}
.
We will call a component (
3.8
) in the intersection of paths
\pi_{i}
,
\pi_{j}
,
a
crossing
of
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{j}
if the two paths enter
x_{k}
and exit
x_{\ell}
in different orders.
Extending the Billey–Warrington definition of defect to accommodate three or more paths passing through a vertex, we have the following
[
11
, §5]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Given star network
F\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
having internal vertices
x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc,
and
path family
\pi
covering
F
,
define a
defect of
\pi
at

{x}_{k}
to be
a triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
with

i<j

and
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{j}
meeting at
{x}_{k}
after having crossed an odd number of times.
Define
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)
to be the number of defects of
\pi
.

Observation 3.2
.

A defect
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
of a path family
\pi
must satisfy
k\geq 2
.

We may state an alternative formulation of the defect statistic in terms of the natural vertical ordering of edges entering an internal vertex
x_{k}
of a planar network.
This ordering induces relations
\prec_{k}
,
\sim_{k}
,
\precsim_{k}
on paths which pass through
x_{k}
. While these relations depend upon the index
k
, we economize notation by omitting
k
when the vertex
x_{k}
is clear from context.
We declare that
\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{j}
if
\pi_{i}
enters
x_{k}
below
\pi_{j}
,
that
\pi_{i}\sim\pi_{j}

if
\pi_{i},\pi_{j}
enter
x_{k}
on the same edge,
and that
\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{j}
if

\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{j}
or
\pi_{i}\sim\pi_{j}
.
For example, consider the path family

(3.9)

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle 4
\scriptstyle 4
\scriptstyle(2)
\scriptstyle x_{1}
\scriptstyle x_{2}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

and the
paths entering the vertex
x_{2}
.
In terms of this vertex we have

\pi_{2}\sim\pi_{3},\quad\pi_{2}\prec\pi_{4},\quad\pi_{3}\prec\pi_{4}.

Observation 3.3
.

The relations
\prec
,
\sim
,
\precsim
defined in terms of any fixed internal vertex of a star network
F

are a (strict) partial order, an equivalence relation, and a preorder, respectively, on source-to-sink paths in
F
.
These relations satisfy

(1)

\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{j}\sim\pi_{\ell}
implies
\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{\ell}
,

(2)

\pi_{i}\sim\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{\ell}
implies
\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{\ell}
,

(3)

\pi_{i}\not\prec\pi_{j}
is equivalent to
\pi_{j}\precsim\pi_{i}
.

In terms of the partial order
\prec=\prec_{k}
, we can define a defect at vertex
x_{k}
as follows.

Lemma 3.4
.

For star network
F\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
and path family
\pi\in\Pi(F)
, the triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
is a defect if and only if
i<j
and
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}
.

Proof.

Write
F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
. Assuming
\pi_{i},\pi_{j}
with
i<j
meet at
{x}_{k}
, we have that
\pi_{i}
enters
{x}_{k}

above
\pi_{j}
if and only if the two paths have crossed an odd number of times in
F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots F_{[a_{k-1},b_{k-1}]}
.
∎

Extending the algebraic interpretation (
2.7
) of a planar network, we define the set

(3.10)

\Pi_{v,d}(F)=\{\pi\in\Pi_{v}(F)\,|\,\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)=d\}

and we
say that
F

graphically
represents

(3.11)

\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(F)|q^{d}T_{v}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}

as an element of
H_{n}(q)
.
For
F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
, this element is

(3.12)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}}

[
11
, Cor. 5.3]
,
[
24
, Prop. 4.2]
;
for
F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
, it is
(
3.12
) divided by
the product, over all pairs
i<j
, of the

q
-factorial polynomials

\mu(x_{i},x_{j})_{q}!
,
where we define
\mu(x_{i},x_{j})
as in (
2.5
) and

[p]_{q}:=\begin{cases}1+q+\cdots+q^{p-1}&\text{if $p>0$},\\
0&\text{if $p=0$},\end{cases}\qquad\quad[p]_{q}!:=\begin{cases}[1]_{q}[2]_{q}\cdots[p]_{q}&\text{if $p>0$},\\
1&\text{if $p=0$}.\end{cases}

(See, e.g.,
[
27
]
.)
Summarizing results in
[
11
]
,
[
24
]
, we prove the second fact as follows.

Proposition 3.5
.

Fix
F\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
, assume that
p>1

multiplicity-
1
edges are incident upon some pair of internal vertices, and construct
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
by replacing these edges by one edge of multiplicity
p
.
Then
F^{\prime}
graphically represents the element

\frac{1}{[p]_{q}!}\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(F)}\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}.

Proof.

Let
{x}_{r}
,
{x}_{s}
be the pair of internal vertices in
F
, let
e_{1},\dots,e_{p}
be the edges incident upon both vertices
in
F
, and let
e
be the multiplicity-
p
edge replacing these in
F^{\prime}
.
Thus for every path family
in
\Pi(F)
,

p
of its component paths contain the edges
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{p}
,
and for every path family in
\Pi(F^{\prime})
,

p
of its component paths contain the edge
e
.
Define the map

\theta:\Pi(F)\rightarrow\Pi(F^{\prime})

by replacing edges
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{p}
in each path family
\pi\in\Pi(F)
by
p
copies of the edge
e
.

It is clear that
the map
\theta
is surjective and that
for each path family
\sigma\in\Pi(F^{\prime})
,
the set
\theta^{-1}(\sigma)\subseteq\Pi(F)
consists of
p!
path families
\pi
satisfying
\mathrm{type}(\pi)=\mathrm{type}(\sigma)
.
We claim that the defects of
path families in each preimage
\theta^{-1}(\sigma)
satisfy

(3.13)

\sum_{\pi\in\theta^{-1}(\sigma)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}=[p]_{q}!q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)}.

To see this, fix
\pi\in\theta^{-1}(\sigma)
and observe that for all internal vertices
{x}_{t}\neq{x}_{s}
of
F
and
F^{\prime}
, the triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},t)
is defective if and only if
(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j},t)
is defective:
the agreement of
\pi
and
\sigma
outside of the subgraph induced by
{x}_{r}
,
{x}_{s}
guarantees that
the preorder
\prec_{t}
defined in terms of any internal vertex
{x}_{t}
satisfies

\pi_{i}\prec_{t}\pi_{j}
if and only if
\sigma_{i}\prec_{t}\sigma_{j}
. It follows that the difference between
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)
and

\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)
is just the number of defects of
\pi
at
{x}_{s}
.
Letting
\pi_{u_{1}},\dotsc,\pi_{u_{p}}
be the
p
paths passing through
{x}_{r}
,
{x}_{s}
, labeled by

\pi_{u_{1}}\prec_{s}\cdots\prec_{s}\pi_{u_{p}},

we have that the number of defects of
\pi
at
{x}_{s}

equals the number of inversions in the word
u_{1}\cdots u_{p}
.
Thus we have

\sum_{\pi\in\theta^{-1}(\sigma)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}=\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}}q^{\textsc{inv}(u)+\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)},

which implies (
3.13
).

It follows that

F^{\prime}
graphically represents the element

(3.14)

\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi(F^{\prime})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\sigma)}=\negthickspace\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi(F^{\prime})}\frac{1}{[p]_{q}!}\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\theta^{-1}(\sigma)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\sigma)}.

Since
\mathrm{type}(\pi)=\mathrm{type}(\sigma)

for all
\pi\in\theta^{-1}(\sigma)
, and since

\Pi(F)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Pi(F^{\prime})}\negthickspace\negthickspace\theta^{-1}(\sigma),

we have the desired result.
∎

The product of polynomials
\mu(x_{i},x_{j})_{q}!

mentioned after (
3.12
) also can be expressed in terms of intervals appearing in reversals
[
24
, Thm. 4.3]
.
Given a sequence of
m
intervals

(3.15)

\mathcal{A}=([a_{1},b_{1}],\dotsc,[a_{m},b_{m}]),

define
\tbinom{m}{2}
more intervals
\{I_{i,j}\,|\,i<j\}
by

(3.16)

I_{i,j}=[a_{i},b_{i}]\cap[a_{j},b_{j}]\smallsetminus([a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{j-1},b_{j-1}]).

Let
f_{\negthinspace\mathcal{A}}(q)
be the product of the
q
-factorials of the cardinalities of the intervals (
3.16
),

(3.17)

f_{\negthinspace\mathcal{A}}(q):=\prod_{i<j}|I_{i,j}|_{q}!.

Say that a Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{w}
has a
reversal factorization
if there is a sequence (
3.15
) of intervals satisfying

(3.18)

\widetilde{C}_{w}=\frac{1}{f_{\negthinspace\mathcal{A}}(q)}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}}.

This property of a Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{w}
guarantees the following combinatorial interpretation of the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
\{P_{v,w}(q)\mid v\leq w\}

[
11
, Cor. 5.3]
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\widetilde{C}_{w}
have the reversal factorization (
3.18
) and define the planar network
F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
.
Then we have

(3.19)

P_{v,w}(q)=\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(F)|q^{d}.

Proof.

Let
G=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
. By
[
11
, Cor. 5.3]
, we have that the coefficient of
T_{w}
in
\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}}
is

\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(G)|q^{d}.

By Proposition
3.5
, it follows that the network
F

and polynomial
f_{\negthinspace\mathcal{A}}(q)
(
3.17
) satisfy

\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(F)|q^{d}=\frac{1}{f_{\negthinspace\mathcal{A}}(q)}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(G)|q^{d}.

∎

Two results providing sufficient conditions on
w
for a reversal factorization of
\widetilde{C}_{w}
are the following
[
4
, Thm. 1]
,
[
24
, Thm. 4.3]
.

Theorem 3.7
.

If
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoids the patterns
321
,
56781234
,
46781235
,
56718234
,
46718235
,
and if
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
is a reduced expression for
w
, then we have

\widetilde{C}_{w}=\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{1}}}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{\ell}}}
.

Theorem 3.8
.

If
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
, then
\widetilde{C}_{w}
has a reversal factorization.

The combination of Theorems
3.7
,
3.8
is not the strongest factorization result possible.
Indeed, the known reversal factorization
\widetilde{C}_{4231}=\widetilde{C}_{s_{[1,2]}}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[2,4]}}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[1,2]}}
is guaranteed by neither theorem. We therefore have the following question
[
24
, Quest. 4.5]
.

Question 3.9
.

For which
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
does
\widetilde{C}_{w}
have a reversal factorization?

4.
Reduction of defects

Our defect reduction theorem, Theorem
4.9
,
asserts that if a star network can be covered by a path family having
d
defects, then it can also be covered by a path family of the same type having
d-1
defects. In certain simple cases, we can easily produce a
(d-1)
-defect family in some set
\Pi_{v}(F)
from a
d
-defect family in
\Pi_{v}(F)
by swapping a pair of subpaths.
For example, consider the
star network and path families

(4.1)

with
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)=1
, and
\sigma
constructed from
\pi
by
swapping the
x_{2}
-to-
x_{4}
subpaths of

\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{4}
. The swap
eliminates the defect
(\pi_{1},\pi_{4},4)
,
but introduces two more:

(4.3)

(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{4},3),\quad(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},3).

Thus we have
\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)=2
.
There is in fact a path family

(4.4)

\tau=\hbox to78.94pt{\vbox to55.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.01653pt\lower 6.75255pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

of type
1342
satisfying

\mathrm{dfct}(\tau)=\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)-1=0
, but the naive approach above does not produce it from
\pi
.

To describe the process of reducing defects in a path family by exactly
1
, we begin
by stating a map which modifies a path family by removing a defect at a specified vertex,
possibly creating earlier defects.
For

(4.5)

F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\cdots F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}\quad\text{having internal vertices }{x}_{1},\dotsc,{x}_{m}

and
k\in\{2,\dotsc,m\}
,
define

(4.6)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}:\{\pi\in\Pi(F)\,|\,\pi\text{ has a defect at }x_{k}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Pi(F)

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\mapsto\hat{\pi}

by the following algorithm.

Algorithm 4.1
.

Given star network

F
as in (
4.5
),
path family
\pi\in\Pi(F)
,
and index
k
such that
\pi
has a defect at
{x}_{k}
,
do

(1)

Let (
r,t
) be the lexicographically least pair such that
(\pi_{r},\pi_{t},k)
is a defect.

(2)

Let
s
be the largest index such that
\pi_{s}
enters vertex
{x}_{k}

through the same edge as
\pi_{r}
and
(\pi_{s},\pi_{t},k)
is a defect.

(3)

Let
{x}_{\ell}
be the final vertex in the rightmost crossing of

\pi_{s}
and
\pi_{t}
prior to
{x}_{k}
.

(4)

Create a new path family
\hat{\pi}=(\hat{\pi}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{\pi}_{n})
by

(a)

\hat{\pi}_{i}=\pi_{i}
if
i\not\in\{s,t\}
.

(b)

\hat{\pi}_{s}
is
\pi_{s}
with the

{x}_{\ell}
-to-
{x}_{k}
subpath replaced by that of
\pi_{t}
.

(c)

\hat{\pi}_{t}
is
\pi_{t}
with the

{x}_{\ell}
-to-
{x}_{k}
subpath replaced by that of
\pi_{s}
.

For example, applying
\phi_{3}
to the path family
\sigma
in (
4.2
), we consider the defects (
4.3
) and set
(r,t)=(2,4)
. Now we consider the paths entering
{x}_{3}
through the same edge as
\sigma_{2}
and also creating a defect at
{x}_{3}
with
\sigma_{4}
. Of these,
\sigma_{3}
has the greatest index and we set
s=3
. Prior to
{x}_{3}
, the rightmost crossing of
\sigma_{3}
and
\sigma_{4}
is the vertex
{x}_{1}
, so we set
\ell=1
. We create a new path family

\hat{\sigma}=(\hat{\sigma}_{1},\hat{\sigma}_{2},\hat{\sigma}_{3},\hat{\sigma}_{4})
by swapping the
{x}_{1}
-to-
{x}_{3}
subpaths of
\sigma_{3}
and
\sigma_{4}
,

\hat{\sigma}=\phi_{3}(\sigma)=\hbox to78.94pt{\vbox to55.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.01653pt\lower 6.75255pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.451pt}{48.78539pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.12079pt}{11.08556pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.79057pt}{48.78539pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{44.86218pt}{11.08556pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We leave as an exercise for the reader the verification that the path families
\pi,\sigma,\hat{\sigma},\tau
are related by
\sigma=\phi_{4}(\pi)
,
\tau=\phi_{3}(\hat{\sigma})
.

It is straightforward to verify that the path families
\pi
,
\hat{\pi}
in Algorithm
4.1
have the same type, and that their defects at
x_{k+1},\dotsc,x_{m}
correspond bijectively.

Proposition 4.2
.

Given star network

F
as in (
4.5
),
path family
\pi\in\Pi(F)

and index
k
such that
\pi
has a defect at
{x}_{k}
,
the path family
\hat{\pi}=\phi_{k}(\pi)
satisfies

(1)

\mathrm{type}(\hat{\pi})=\mathrm{type}(\pi)
,

(2)

for each
p>k
, we have
\{(i,j)\,|\,(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},p)\text{ defective}\}=\{(i,j)\,|\,(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},p)\text{ defective}\}
.

Proof.

(1) Since
\hat{\pi}
differs from
\pi
only in subpaths between interior vertices
{x}_{\ell}
and
x_{k}
, we have that for
i=1,\dotsc,n
,
path
\hat{\pi}_{i}
has the same source and sink vertices as
\pi_{i}
.

(2) Since the restrictions of
\hat{\pi}

and
\pi
to
F_{[a_{k+1},b_{k+1}]}\cdots F_{[a_{m},b_{m}]}
are identical, we have for each triple
(i,j,p)
with
i<j
and
p>k
that
\pi_{i}
enters
{x}_{p}
above
\pi_{j}
if and only if
\hat{\pi}_{i}
enters
{x}_{p}
above
\hat{\pi}_{j}
.
By Lemma
3.4
,
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},p)
is defective if and only if
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},p)
is.
∎

On the other hand, path families
\pi
,
\hat{\pi}

need not have a bijective correspondence of defects occuring before
x_{k}
.
For example in (
4.2
) – (
4.3
), the path family
\pi
has 0 defects at
{x}_{3}
while
\sigma=\phi_{4}(\pi)
has 2.

We will show in Proposition
4.7
that

\hat{\pi}
has exactly one fewer defect at
x_{k}
than
\pi
does. The “extra” defect of
\pi
is easy to describe.
Let

s
,
t

be the numbers computed in steps 1 – 2 of
Algorithm
4.1
and order the paths entering
{x}_{k}
by
\prec
as in Section 3.
Then

s
,
t
satisfy

(4.7)

s<t,\qquad\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{s},\qquad\hat{\pi}_{s}\prec\hat{\pi}_{t},

and by Lemma
3.4
, the triple

(\pi_{s},\pi_{t},k)
is defective and the triple
(\hat{\pi}_{s},\hat{\pi}_{t},k)
is not.
We claim that other defects of
\pi
at
{x}_{k}
correspond bijectively to all defects of
\hat{\pi}
at
{x}_{k}
.
First, it is easy to see that for
i,j\not\in\{s,t\}
, the triple

(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
is defective if and only if the triple
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
is:
in this case we have
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\pi_{i}
and
\hat{\pi}_{j}=\pi_{j}
.
Next, we look closely
at defects
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
and
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)

with exactly one of the indices
\{i,j\}
belonging to
\{s,t\}
,

(4.8)

|\{i,j\}\cap\{s,t\}|=1,

and show that these correspond bijectively. Call these sets of defects
\mathcal{D}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})
, respectively,
and define four subsets of each:

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle:=\{(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi)\,|\,\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{t}\},\qquad\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:=\{(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})\,|\,\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{s}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle:=\{(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi)\,|\,\pi_{s}\prec\pi_{i}\},\qquad\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:=\{(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})\,|\,\hat{\pi}_{t}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi)\,|\,\pi_{t}\precsim\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}=\pi_{s}\text{ and }\pi_{t}\neq\pi_{j}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}:=\{(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})\,|\,\hat{\pi}_{s}\precsim\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}=\hat{\pi}_{t}\text{ and }\hat{\pi}_{s}\neq\hat{\pi}_{j}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\{(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi)\,|\,\pi_{t}=\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{s}\text{ and }\pi_{i}\neq\pi_{s}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}:=\{(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})\,|\,\hat{\pi}_{s}=\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}\precsim\hat{\pi}_{t}\text{ and }\hat{\pi}_{i}\neq\hat{\pi}_{t}\}.

Lemma 4.3
.

The sets (
4.9
) partition
\mathcal{D}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})
into disjoint blocks:

(4.10)

\mathcal{D}(\pi)=\mathcal{A}\uplus\mathcal{B}\uplus\mathcal{C}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{C}_{2},\qquad\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\uplus\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\uplus\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}.

Proof.

To see the first equality of (
4.10
), observe that by definition of
\mathcal{D}(\pi)
, we have

(4.11)

i<j,\qquad\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}.

Consider a defect
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi)
. If
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{t}
, the defect belongs to
\mathcal{A}
. If
\pi_{s}\prec\pi_{i}
, it belongs to
\mathcal{B}
. On the other hand, we cannot have
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{t}
and
\pi_{s}\prec\pi_{i}
simultaneously: by (
4.7
) and (
4.11
), this implies
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{s}\prec\pi_{i}
, contradicting (
4.8
). Thus we have
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
. Now suppose we have
\pi_{j}\not\prec\pi_{t}
and
\pi_{s}\not\prec\pi_{i}
, i.e.,
\pi_{t}\precsim\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{s}
. By (
4.8
), this implies that either
\pi_{t}\precsim\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}=\pi_{s}\text{ and }\pi_{t}\neq\pi_{j}
, or
\pi_{t}=\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{s}\text{ and }\pi_{i}\neq\pi_{s}
, and the defect belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
or
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, respectively.

The second equality of (
4.10
) is similar.
∎

To demonstrate the claimed bijection between
\mathcal{D}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})
, we define the map

(4.12)

\displaystyle\psi:\mathcal{D}(\pi)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi}),

\displaystyle(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k),\text{ if }(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\\
(\hat{\pi}_{t},\hat{\pi}_{j},k),\text{ if }(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)=(\pi_{s},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{C}_{1},\\
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{s},k),\text{ if }(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)=(\pi_{i},\pi_{t},k)\in\mathcal{C}_{2},\end{cases}

and prove that it is bijective.

Lemma 4.4
.

The map
\psi
is well defined: for
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
defective, we have that

\psi(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
is also defective. Furthermore, we have

\psi(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,

\psi(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,

\psi(\mathcal{C}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
,

\psi(\mathcal{C}_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Proof.

For
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{A}
, the conditions (
4.7
) imply that we have

\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{s}
and
i\in\{s,t\}
.
By Algorithm
4.1
, the edges on which
\hat{\pi}_{s}
,
\hat{\pi}_{t}
enter
x_{k}
are those on which

\pi_{t}
,
\pi_{s}
enter
x_{k}
, respectively.
Thus we have
\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{s}\prec\hat{\pi}_{t}
, implying that
\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}
, and

\psi(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
is defective.
In particular, we have

(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Similarly, for
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{B}
we have that
\psi(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)

is defective and
belongs to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

Now let
r
be the number computed in step
1
of Algorithm
4.1
, satisfying

(4.13)

r\leq s<t,\qquad\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{r},\pi_{s},\qquad\pi_{r}\sim\pi_{s}.

For
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
the conditions

\pi_{t}\precsim\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}=\pi_{s}
and
\pi_{t}\neq\pi_{j}

and Algorithm
4.1

guarantee that
we have

\hat{\pi}_{s}\precsim\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{t}
.
To see that
\psi(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)=(\hat{\pi}_{t},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
is defective and belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
, it remains to show that
t<j
and

\hat{\pi}_{s}\neq\hat{\pi}_{j}
.
Since we have
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}=\pi_{s}\sim\pi_{r}
with
r\leq s=i<j
, we know that
(\pi_{r},\pi_{j},k)
is defective and that
\hat{\pi}_{s}\neq\hat{\pi}_{j}
.
By our choice of
r
in step
1
of Algorithm
4.1
, we must have
(r,t)\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}(r,j)
and therefore
t\leq j
. Since
\pi_{t}\neq\pi_{j}
, we
have
t<j
, as desired.

For
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
, the conditions

\pi_{t}=\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{s}
and
\pi_{i}\neq\pi_{s}

and
Algorithm
4.1

guarantee that
we have
\hat{\pi}_{s}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}\precsim\hat{\pi}_{t}
and
\hat{\pi}_{i}\neq\hat{\pi}_{t}
.
To see that
\psi(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{s},k)
is defective and belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
,
it remains to show that
i<s
.
Since
\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{s}
and by (
4.13
),
\pi_{i},\pi_{s},\pi_{r}
are related by
\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{s}\sim\pi_{r}
or
\pi_{i}\sim\pi_{s}\sim\pi_{r}
.
Consider the first case. We have
i<j=t
so
(r,i)<_{\mathrm{lex}}(r,t)
, and by step 1 of Algorithm
4.1

(\pi_{r},\pi_{i},k)
is not defective. We know that
\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{r}
so
i<r
. By (
4.13
) we also have
r\leq s
, and thus
i<s
.
Now consider the second case. Since
\pi_{i}\sim\pi_{r}
and
(\pi_{i},\pi_{t},k)
is defective, step 2 of Algorithm
4.1
gives
i<s
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

The map
\psi
is injective.

Proof.

By Lemmas
4.3
–
4.4
, we consider pairs
(\pi_{i_{1}},\pi_{j_{1}},k),(\pi_{i_{2}},\pi_{j_{2}},k)
of distinct defects in
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
separately.

If the two defects belong to
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}
, then
\psi
clearly maps them
to
(\hat{\pi}_{i_{1}},\hat{\pi}_{j_{1}},k)\neq(\hat{\pi}_{i_{2}},\hat{\pi}_{j_{2}},k)
.
If the two defects belong to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, then we have

i_{1}=i_{2}=s
, so
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
. Thus
\psi
maps the two defects to
(\hat{\pi}_{t},\hat{\pi}_{j_{1}},k)\neq(\hat{\pi}_{t},\hat{\pi}_{j_{2}},k)
.
Finally, if the two defects belong to
\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
then we have

j_{1}=j_{2}=t
, so
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
. Thus
\psi
maps the two defects to
(\hat{\pi}_{i_{1}},\hat{\pi}_{s},k)\neq(\hat{\pi}_{i_{2}},\hat{\pi}_{s},k)
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

The map
\psi
is surjective.

Proof.

For
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

we have
\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{s}\prec\hat{\pi}_{t}
,
i\in\{s,t\}
, so
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{s}
. Since
i<j
, it follows that
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
is defective, belongs to
\mathcal{A}
, and satisfies

\psi((\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k))=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
.
Similarly, for

(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

we have that

(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
is defective, belongs to
\mathcal{B}
, and satisfies

\psi((\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k))=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
.

For
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
we have

\hat{\pi}_{s}\precsim\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}=\hat{\pi}_{t}
with
\hat{\pi}_{s}\neq\hat{\pi}_{j}
.
Consider the triple
(\pi_{s},\pi_{j},k)
.
We have that
s<t=i<j
.
This means that
\hat{\pi}_{j}=\pi_{j}
so
\pi_{t}\precsim\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{s}
. Since
s<j
and
\pi_{j}\prec\pi_{s}
, we have that
(\pi_{s},\pi_{j},k)
is a defect. Furthermore, these conditions imply that we have
(\pi_{s},\pi_{j},k)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
By (
4.12
),
\psi((\pi_{s},\pi_{j},k))=(\hat{\pi}_{t},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
.

For
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
we have

\hat{\pi}_{s}=\hat{\pi}_{j}\prec\hat{\pi}_{i}\precsim\hat{\pi}_{t}
with
\hat{\pi}_{t}\neq\hat{\pi}_{i}
. Consider the triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{t},k)
. We have that
i<j=s<t
. This means that
\hat{\pi}_{i}=\pi_{i}
so
\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{i}\precsim\pi_{s}
. Since
i<t
and
\pi_{t}\prec\pi_{i}
, we have that
(\pi_{i},\pi_{t},k)
is a defect. Furthermore, these conditions imply that we have
(\pi_{i},\pi_{t},k)\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
. By (
4.12
),
\psi((\pi_{i},\pi_{t},k))=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{s},k)=(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
.
∎

Given the bijectivity of
\psi
, we can now show that the map
\phi_{k}

(
4.6
), designed to modify a path family by removing a defect at
a specified vertex
x_{k}
, indeed does so.

Proposition 4.7
.

Given star network

F
as in (
4.5
),
path family
\pi\in\Pi(F)
,
and index
k
such that
\pi
has a defect at
{x}_{k}
, we have that
\phi_{k}(\pi)=\hat{\pi}
satisfies

\#\{(i,j)\,|\,(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)\text{ defective}\}=\#\{(i,j)\,|\,(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)\text{ defective}\}-1.

Proof.

By Definition
3.1
we consider only pairs
(i,j)
with
i<j
.
Let
s
,
t

be the numbers computed in steps 1 – 2 of
Algorithm
4.1
,
and consider three cases of pairs
(i,j)
.

If
|\{i,j\}\cap\{s,t\}|=0
, then
by the discussion between (
4.7
) and (
4.8
),
we have that
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
is defective
if and only if
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
is defective.

If
|\{i,j\}\cap\{s,t\}|=1
,
then by Lemmas
4.4
–
4.6
,
we have a bijection between the corresponding sets
\mathcal{D}(\pi)
,
\mathcal{D}(\hat{\pi})

of defects.

Finally, if
|\{i,j\}\cap\{s,t\}|=2
,
then by Lemma
3.4
we have that

(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)

is defective while
(\hat{\pi}_{i},\hat{\pi}_{j},k)
is not.
∎

For
F
as in (
4.5
),
repeated application of the map
\phi_{k}
to a path family
\pi\in\Pi_{w}(F)

produces a new path family in
\Pi_{w}(F)

having defects which match those of
\pi
at vertices
x_{k+1},\dotsc,x_{m}
,
having no defects at the vertex
x_{k}
,
and possibly having more defects than
\pi
has at vertices
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k-1}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Fix
F
as in (
4.5
),
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\pi\in\Pi_{v}(F)
, and
k\in[m]
.
Then there exists
\sigma\in\Pi_{v}(F)
satisfying

(1)

for each
p>k
,
\{(i,j)\,|\,(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j},p)\text{ defective}\}=\{(i,j)\,|\,(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},p)\text{ defective}\}
,

(2)

\#\{(i,j)\,|\,(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j},k)\text{ defective}\}=0
.

Proof.

Let
d=\#\{(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},j)\,|\,(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},j)\text{ defective}\}
. Let
\sigma=\phi_{k}^{d}(\pi)
. By Proposition
4.2
, these
d
iterations of Algorithm
4.1
guarantee that we have
\sigma\in\Pi_{v}(F)
and
(1)
. Then by Proposition
4.7
these
d
iterations give
(2)
.
∎

We can now show, as claimed before (
4.1
), that if a star network can be covered by a path family having

d
defects, then it can be covered by another path family of the same
type having
d-1
defects.
This second path family is obtained from the first by judicious applications of the maps
\phi_{1},\dotsc,\phi_{m}
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Fix star network
F
as in (
4.5
). If for some
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
d\geq 1
the set
\Pi_{v,d}(F)
is nonempty, then the sets
\Pi_{v,0}(F),\dotsc,\Pi_{v,d-1}(F)
are also nonempty.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for
\Pi_{v,d}(F)
nonempty, we also have
\Pi_{v,d-1}(F)
nonempty. Take
\pi\in\Pi_{v,d}(F)
. Suppose
k
is the smallest value such that there exists some defect of the form
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
. By Proposition
4.2
, we have that
\hat{\pi}=\phi_{k}(\pi)\in\Pi_{v}(F)
satisfies

(i)

\hat{\pi}\in\Pi_{v}(F)
,

(ii)

\hat{\pi}
and
\pi
have the same numbers of defects at

x_{k+1},\dotsc,x_{m}
,

(iii)

\hat{\pi}
has one fewer defect at
x_{k}
than
\pi
has.

While
\hat{\pi}
may have defects at
x_{2},\dotsc,x_{k-1}
,
we create a new path family by removing these defects from
\hat{\pi}
, one vertex at a time.
Let
\pi^{(k)}=\hat{\pi}
and construct path families

\pi^{(k-1)},\,\pi^{(k-2)},\,\dots,\pi^{(2)}=\sigma

as follows. For
h=k-1,\dots,2
, do

(1)

Let
d_{h}
be the number of defects of the form
(\pi_{i}^{(h+1)},\,\pi_{j}^{(h+1)},\,h)
in
\pi^{(h+1)}
.

(2)

Define
\pi^{(h)}=\phi_{h}^{d_{h}}(\pi^{(h+1)})
.

By Proposition
4.8
, each path family
\pi^{(h)},h=k-1,\dots,2
has type
v
, has no defects at
{x}_{h}
, and has the same number of defects at
{x}_{h+1},\dots,{x}_{m}
as
\pi^{(h+1)}
. It follows that
\sigma=\pi^{(2)}
, like
\pi
, has no defects at
{x}_{2},\dots,{x}_{k-1}
. By Observation
3.2
, neither
\sigma
nor
\pi
has a defect at
{x}_{1}
. Furthermore,
\sigma
has one fewer defect at
{x}_{k}
than
\pi
has, and has exactly the same number of defects at
{x}_{k+1},\dots,{x}_{m}
as
\pi
has. Thus we have
\sigma\in\Pi_{v,d-1}(F)
, as desired.
∎

If a star network
F
can be covered by a path family of type
w
having 0 defects, then this path family is unique.

Corollary 4.10
.

For any fixed
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and star network
F
as in (
4.5
),
the number of path families of type
v
and having
0
defects is at most one. If
v=e
, then this number is exactly one.

Proof.

If
\Pi_{v}(F)
is empty, then clearly no path family of type
v
in
F
has
0
defects. Assume therefore that
\Pi_{v}(F)
is nonempty. By Theorem
4.9
this set contains at least one path family having
0
defects.

Consider the case
v=e
. If
\pi\in\Pi_{e}(F)
has two paths which cross, then those paths must later cross again, creating a defect. Thus there is a unique path family in
\Pi_{e}(F)
having no defects: the path family having no crossings.

Suppose now that the claim is true for

v
of length
0,\dotsc,\ell-1
and consider
v
of length
\ell
.
Let
s_{i}
be a left descent of
v
, i.e.,

s_{i}v<v
, equivalently,
v_{i}>v_{i+1}
.
Choose

\pi\in\Pi_{v,0}(F)

and let
({x}_{p_{1}},\dots,{x}_{p_{2}})
be the unique crossing of

\pi_{i},\pi_{i+1}
.
Define
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{s_{i}v}(F)

by swapping
{x}_{p_{2}}
-to-sink subpaths of
\pi_{i},\pi_{i+1}
.
We will show that the map
\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}
is a bijection from
\Pi_{v,0}(F)
to
\Pi_{s_{i}v,0}(F)
. By induction, the uniqueness of
\pi\in\Pi_{v,0}(F)
will follow.

First we claim that
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi^{\prime})=0
. To see this, suppose that
(\pi^{\prime}_{h},\pi^{\prime}_{j},q)
is a defect of
\pi^{\prime}
and consider the sets
\{h,j\},\{i,i+1\}
.
The sets cannot be equal since
\pi^{\prime}_{i},\pi^{\prime}_{i+1}
do not cross.
On the other hand, the sets cannot be disjoint, since the equalities
\pi_{h}=\pi^{\prime}_{h}
and
\pi_{j}=\pi^{\prime}_{j}
would imply a defect in
\pi
. We therefore have

(4.14)

h<i\text{ and }j\in\{i,i+1\}\quad\text{or}\quad h\in\{i,i+1\}\text{ and }i+1<j.

If
h<i=j
, then
(\pi_{h}^{\prime},\pi_{i}^{\prime},q)
is a defect of
\pi^{\prime}
. By Lemma
3.4
we have
\pi^{\prime}_{i}\prec\pi^{\prime}_{h}
. If
q\leq p_{2}
then this defect in
\pi^{\prime}
implies the triple
(\pi_{h},\pi_{i},q)
to be defective in
\pi
;
if
q>p_{2}
, then
it follows that
\pi_{i+1}\prec\pi_{h}
and
(\pi_{h},\pi_{i+1},q)
is defective in
\pi
.
If on the other hand
h<i
and
i+1=j
, then
(\pi_{h}^{\prime},\pi_{i+1}^{\prime},q)
is a defect of
\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{i+1}\prec\pi^{\prime}_{h}
. Again, we have
q>p_{2}
and it follows that
\pi_{i}\prec\pi_{h}
. By Lemma
3.4
,
(\pi_{h},\pi_{i},q)
is a defect of
\pi
. Since
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)=0
, all of these cases lead to a contradiction. For the remaining cases in (
4.14
), the argument is similar.

Now we claim that the map
\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}
from
\Pi_{v,0}(F)
to
\Pi_{s_{i}v,0}(F)
is invertible:
its inverse is given by finding the rightmost component
({x}_{p_{1}},\dots,{x}_{p_{2}})
of intersection of
\pi_{i}^{\prime},\pi_{i+1}^{\prime}
and making it into a crossing. The lack of defects in
\pi
implies that
({x}_{p_{1}},\dots,{x}_{p_{2}})
is the rightmost component of intersection of
\pi_{i},\pi_{i+1}
as well. By the inductive hypothesis,
\pi^{\prime}
is the unique path family in
\Pi_{s_{i}v,0}(F)
, so
\pi
is the unique path family in
\Pi_{v,0}(F)
.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Fix star network
F
as in (
4.5
).
If
\Pi_{e}(F)
contains more than one path family, then it contains a path family having exactly one defect.

Proof.

Suppose
\Pi_{e}(F)
contains at least two path families.
By Corollary
4.10
, one element of
\Pi_{e}(F)
is the unique
element of
\Pi_{e,0}(F)
.
Choose another path family in
\Pi_{e}(F)
and let
d\geq 1
be the
number of its defects. By Theorem
4.9
,
the set
\Pi_{e,1}(F)
is nonempty.
∎

5.
Main Result

Every polynomial in
\mathbb{N}[q]
with constant term
1
arises as a Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomial
[
23
]
. Gaetz–Gao
[
15
]
studied the sequences of coefficients in these polynomials, especially coefficients equal to
0

between other
nonzero coefficients.

Define a function
\mathrm{singdeg}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
by

(5.1)

\mathrm{singdeg}(w)=\begin{cases}\min\{k>0\,|\,\text{coefficient of $q^{k}$ in $P_{e,w}(q)$ is nonzero}\}&\text{if $P_{e,w}(q)\neq 1$},\\
\infty&\text{if $P_{e,w}(q)=1$}.\end{cases}

This is a lower bound on degrees for which
Poincaré duality fails in the Schubert variety
X_{w}

[
21
]
, and can be computed in terms of patterns in
w
and a related definition.
Specifically, given
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

not avoiding the pattern
3412
,
define the 3412-
gap
of
w

by

(5.2)

\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(w)=\min\{w_{i_{1}}-w_{i_{4}}\,|\,\text{subword $w_{i_{1}}w_{i_{2}}w_{i_{3}}w_{i_{4}}$
matches the pattern $3412$}\}.

For
w
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, we have
\mathrm{singdeg}(w)=\infty
. Otherwise, we can compute
\mathrm{singdeg}(w)
in terms of
\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(w)
as follows

[
15
, Thm. 1.6]
.

Theorem 5.1
.

For
w
not avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231

we have

\mathrm{singdeg}(w)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(w)&\text{if $w$ avoids the pattern $4231$},\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For example, consider the permutation
45312\in\mathfrak{S}_{5}
, which
avoids the pattern
4231
and has
only the subword
4512
matching the pattern
3412
. Since
\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(45312)=2
, Theorem
5.1
implies that the coefficient of
q
in
P_{e,45312}(q)
is
0
and that the coefficient of
q^{2}
is not.
This is consistent with the fact that
P_{e,45312}(q)=1+q^{2}
. (See
[
5
, p. 75]
.)

For each permutation
w
having
\mathrm{singdeg}(w)>1
,
the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\smash{\widetilde{C}_{w}}
has no reversal factorization
(
3.18
) and therefore is not graphically representable
by a star network, in the sense of (
3.11
).

Theorem 5.2
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

avoiding the pattern
4231
,
not avoiding the pattern
3412
,
and having
\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(w)>1
,
the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{w}

has no reversal factorization.

Proof.

Fix
w
as above with
k=\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(w)
and suppose that the star network
F

graphically represents
\smash{\widetilde{C}_{w}}
as an element of
H_{n}(q)
,

(5.3)

\widetilde{C}_{w}=\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(F)|q^{d}T_{v}=\sum_{v\leq w}P_{v,w}(q)T_{v}.

Since the constant term of
P_{e,w}(q)
is
1
,
we have
|\Pi_{e,0}(F)|=1
.
By our definition of
k

and Theorem
5.1
, the coefficients of
q,\dotsc,q^{k-1}

in
P_{e,w}(q)
are
0
, while the coefficient of
q^{k}
is positive.
In particular, we have the cardinalities
|\Pi_{e,1}(F)|=\cdots=|\Pi_{e,k-1}(F)|=0
and
|\Pi_{e,k}(F)|>0
, which
contradict
Theorem
4.9
.
∎

By Theorem
4.9
, reversal factorization of
\widetilde{C}_{w}
implies that
none
of the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
P_{v,w}(q)
has internal coefficients equal to zero.

Theorem 5.3
.

If
\widetilde{C}_{w}
has a reversal factorization,
then for every
v<w
there exists
k=k(v)

in
\mathbb{N}

such that we have
P_{v,w}(q)=1+a_{1}q+\cdots+a_{k}q^{k}
with
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{k}>0
.

It is easy to show that the inequality
\mathrm{gap_{3412}}(w)>1
implies that
w
does not avoid the pattern
45312
. It is also easy to show that no star network graphically represents

\widetilde{C}_{453129786}
, even though the subword
9786
matches the pattern
4231
. Indeed, some limited experimentation
[
12
]
suggests that avoidance of
the pattern
45312
is important and avoidance of the pattern
4231
is unimportant in the classification of permutations
w
for which

\smash{\widetilde{C}_{w}}
is graphically representable by a star network.
We conjecture the following partial answer to
[
24
, Quest. 4.5]
.

Conjecture 5.4
.

If
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
does not avoid the pattern
45312
, then the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{w}
has no reversal factorization.

For small
n
, it is possible to explicitly enumerate the permutations
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the pattern
45312
and to verify that each corresponding Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{w}
has a reversal factorization
[
12
]
.
Furthermore, given
planar network
F
which graphically represents

\widetilde{C}_{w}
, the reflection of
F
in a horizontal line,
the reflection of
F
in a vertical line,
and the rotation of
F
by
180^{\circ}
can graphically represent up to three additional elements

\widetilde{C}_{w_{0}ww_{0}}
,
\widetilde{C}_{w^{-1}}
,
\widetilde{C}_{w_{0}w^{-1}w_{0}}
,
respectively, where
w_{0}
is the reversal
s_{[1,n]}
.
For example, when
n=5
, all Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements except for
\widetilde{C}_{45312}
have reversal factorizations (which need not be unique).
If
w
avoids the pattern
321
, a factorization of
\widetilde{C}_{w}
is given by Theorem
3.7
.
If
w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
, a factorization of
\widetilde{C}_{w}
is given by
Theorem
3.8
. For the remaining twenty-three permutations
w
, we have the following
factorizations and
graphical representations of
\widetilde{C}_{w}
.

w
